                                           From Good Starts to Optimal Inference: Generalized Latent Factor
                                                 Models with Missingness and Implicit Regularization
                                                                           Chengzhu Huang                  Yuqi Gu

                                                                    Department of Statistics, Columbia University
arXiv:2609.11740v1 [math.ST] 10 Sep 2026




                                                                                           Abstract
                                                    Generalized latent factor models provide a flexible framework for analyzing high-dimensional
                                                non-Gaussian data, but principled estimation and uncertainty quantification under missingness
                                                remain substantially less developed. We develop a theory that connects a computationally
                                                tractable nonconvex procedure directly to statistical inference for nonlinear latent factor models
                                                with exponential-family links and partially observed entries. Our procedure combines a link-
                                                aware double-SVD initialization, a unilateral refinement that achieves rowwise consistency, and
                                                vanilla gradient descent. We show that the refined initializer enters a region of incoherence
                                                and contraction and that gradient descent remains in this region through implicit regulariza-
                                                tion, contracting rapidly down to the statistical estimation error without explicit incoherence or
                                                balancing regularization. Our central result is a uniform rowwise linear approximation for the
                                                actual output of gradient descent that isolates the leading score fluctuations from higher-order
                                                estimation and optimization errors. These expansions yield asymptotically valid individual and
                                                Gaussian multiplier-bootstrap simultaneous inference for latent factors, together with simulta-
                                                neous confidence bands for missing-entry means, without requiring an additional debiasing step.
                                                The resulting estimation rate matches a restricted-class minimax lower bound up to logarithmic
                                                factors, while the theory accommodates severe missingness, weak low-rank signals, and dimin-
                                                ishing local curvature. Simulations support the theoretical findings, and an application to large
                                                language model evaluation illustrates uncertainty-aware estimation and ranking of latent model
                                                capabilities.

                                           Keywords: Generalized latent factor models; High-dimensional inference; Uncertainty quantifica-
                                           tion; Missing data; Nonconvex optimization; Implicit regularization; Nonlinear matrix completion.


                                           1    Introduction
                                           Latent factor models (Bartholomew et al., 2008) have found widespread use in the social sciences,
                                           psychology, recommendation systems, and many other fields. When linear models are inadequate
                                           for heterogeneous discrete observations, generalized latent factor models (Bartholomew et al., 2008;
                                           Skrondal and Rabe-Hesketh, 2004; Huber et al., 2004; Kidzinski et al., 2022; Chen and Li, 2024)
                                           provide a flexible framework for binary, ordinal, and count data. More importantly, amid the
                                           rapid development of large language models, there is a pressing need for trustworthy evaluation
                                           procedures that scale to many models and many questions. Given the binary correct/incorrect
                                           responses of a collection of language models to a common set of questions, latent factor models
                                           offer a powerful way to uncover their underlying capabilities and distinctive strengths.
                                               In many applications, however, only a subset of the data is observed. Survey nonresponse
                                           is often unavoidable, while evaluating every language model on every available question can be


                                                                                                1
prohibitively expensive. Statistical procedures must therefore accommodate missingness in both
latent factor estimation and the subsequent uncertainty quantification.
    For linear low-rank models with missing data, a substantial body of work has developed esti-
mators together with rigorous theoretical guarantees (Candes and Recht, 2009; Chen et al., 2019a;
Xia, 2021). Their extension to generalized latent factor models remains considerably less developed,
even though latent factor models for non-Gaussian data have wide-ranging applications in practice.
More broadly, estimation and inference for nonlinear latent factor models under missingness are
not yet fully understood, nor is the extent to which missingness and sparsity compromise the relia-
bility of the resulting estimates. In this work, we develop statistical foundations for estimation and
uncertainty quantification in generalized latent factor models with partially observed data, with an
emphasis on statistical inference, optimality, and computational tractability.
    To formulate the problem, let R ∈ Rn×p denote the underlying observation matrix, let Ω ⊆
[n]×[p] denote the set of observed locations, and let ζ ∗ ∈ Rp , X∗ ∈ Rn×r , and Y∗ ∈ Rp×r denote the
intercept vector and the left and right factor matrices, respectively. We consider an exponential-
family model under which, conditional on the latent parameters and the observation set Ω, the
likelihood is given by
                                                Y
                   l(R | [ζ ∗ , X∗ , Y∗ ], Ω) =                         ∗        ∗

with Mi,j    j      i   j
in which each entry belongs to Ω independently with probability π, which may decay with the
dimensions n and p. As a concrete example, consider the logistic-link model for binary entries,
under which the responses are conditionally independent and satisfy
                                            exp M ∗

                                 ∗                   i,j
                       Ri,j | Mi,j ∼ Bern                ∗
                                                              , (i, j) ∈ Ω,               (2)
                                             1 + exp Mi,j
and only entries with (i, j) ∈ Ω are observed. This model includes the one-bit matrix completion
problem as a special case (Davenport et al., 2014). As is standard in the latent variable literature,
we impose a low-rank structure through the natural-parameter matrix M∗ . In contrast to the linear
setting, however, this low-rank natural-parameter matrix is observed only indirectly through the
nonlinear conditional-mean map ψ := Ψ′ , which constitutes one main challenge of the problem. In
particular, this nonlinear formulation undermines two main strategies commonly used in prior work
and makes them inapplicable: (i) obtaining the maximizer of (1) in closed form, and (ii) conducting
spectral analysis of the data matrix under a “low-rank signal plus noise” decomposition. Therefore,
this problem calls for a new computational and theoretical framework.
    Existing theory addresses important pieces of this problem separately: inference under miss-
ingness is substantially better developed for linear low-rank models, whereas recent theory for
nonlinear latent factor models often characterizes likelihood-based estimators without establishing
that a computationally tractable algorithm reaches an inferentially regular solution. Our goal is to
bridge these statistical and computational gaps. We show that a fully data-driven procedure can
be initialized in the appropriate local region, that vanilla gradient descent remains there through
implicit regularization and, most importantly, that gradient descent’s actual output admits uniform
rowwise linear expansions that directly enable optimal statistical inference.

1.1   Local Landscape and Downstream Inference
In view of the objective (1), a natural approach is to characterize its global maximizer (Wang, 2022;
Li et al., 2023; Ouyang et al., 2024). This perspective, however, leaves fundamental statistical and

                                                    2
computational issues unresolved. Under severe missingness, the global likelihood maximizer may lie
in a spurious region with poor or even ill-posed statistical properties, while computing it is generally
intractable. We therefore focus on the output of a concrete optimization procedure:gradient descent,
and seek to establish statistical guarantees through an analysis of its optimization dynamics.
    The success of gradient descent hinges on the local geometry of the empirical objective. Under
full observation, suitably regularized likelihoods often inherit the geometry of their population
counterparts. After accounting for the nonidentifiable directions, restricted strong convexity and
smoothness hold throughout an appropriate ℓ2 neighborhood (Ma et al., 2020; Li et al., 2023), with
empirical curvature comparable to its population counterpart along every admissible direction.
Missingness makes this transfer substantially more delicate. The sampling operator can distort the
empirical curvature outside the region of incoherence and introduce spurious local minima even
in linear models. Consequently, proximity in a global norm alone does not guarantee that the
gradient-descent trajectory lies in a region of contraction.
    Prior work on simpler low-rank signal-plus-noise problems has nevertheless revealed an implicit-
regularization phenomenon (Ma et al., 2018; Chen et al., 2019a, 2021c; Chi et al., 2019). Once
initialized in a region of incoherence and contraction, gradient-based methods remain sufficiently
incoherent, encounter population-like curvature, and contract toward the optimal error rate. This
motivates our first question:
      Question 1: Under both nonlinearity and missingness, can one characterize a region
      in which the empirical loss retains population-like curvature and gradient descent con-
      tracts?
   Characterizing such a region is only part of the challenge, since the result becomes operational
only if a data-driven procedure can reach it. This leads to a second question:
      Question 2: Can a computationally tractable initialization scheme reach this region and
      furnish a good start for gradient descent?
The initialization problem is nontrivial. Spectral estimators used for linear low-rank models (Ma
et al., 2018) are no longer suitable because the nonlinear mean matrix generally exhibits rank
inflation and is no longer low-rank. Although link-aware spectral procedures can provide global
recovery guarantees (Ma et al., 2020; Zhang et al., 2020), their existing analyses do not furnish the
rowwise and leave-one-out controls required to study the subsequent gradient-descent trajectory.
    Finally, reaching the region of incoherence and contraction matters not only for optimization.
The stationarity equations at a sufficiently regular solution may yield a tractable stochastic expan-
sion, opening the door to uncertainty quantification for the recovered latent structure. This raises
our third question:
      Question 3: Can one derive a distributional approximation for the output of gradient
      descent and use it to conduct valid statistical inference?

1.2   Linear Approximations for Gradient Descent
We expand on Question 3 in the following. Classical M-estimation provides a useful starting point.
Consider a fixed-dimensional smooth convex problem with random objective Ln , let θ ∗ denote its
population target, and let θ

                                                                                   −1
point enters the region of incoherence and contraction, with − ∇2 E[Ln ](θ ∗ )        ∇Ln (θ ∗ ) serving
as a proxy for θbn − θ ∗ .1 Extending this heuristic to high dimensions is considerably more delicate,
particularly because factor parametrizations are rotationally nonidentifiable. Several recent works
(Wang, 2022; Li et al., 2023; Ouyang et al., 2024) have characterized global minimizers of suitably
regularized maximum likelihood estimators. Our goal is instead to connect tractable computation
directly with valid inference: we aim to derive rowwise linear expansions and distributional approx-
imations for the output of a computationally tractable procedure, while proving that the algorithm
reaches the regime in which these conclusions hold. The resulting conclusions are fundamentally
statistical, but they are accompanied by end-to-end algorithmic guarantees.
    A linear approximation is useful not only for characterizing the fluctuations of individual es-
timates, but also for quantifying uncertainty in the many comparisons that arise in latent factor
analysis. Examples include ranking subjects by latent ability, comparing items by difficulty or dis-
criminatory power, and assessing unobserved entries (Fan et al., 2023, 2025b). Such tasks involve
large collections of dependent contrasts and therefore cannot be justified by pointwise consistency
or marginal normal approximations alone.

1.3    Main Contributions
Building on a nonconvex optimization framework, this work advances the understanding of gra-
dient descent dynamics under both nonlinear observations and missing data, while addressing the
associated initialization challenge through a carefully designed multi-stage pipeline. Beyond esti-
mation, we show that the output of the computational pipeline itself supports optimal individual
and simultaneous statistical inference. Our affirmative answers to Questions 1–3 and technical
contributions are summarized in the following principal contributions.
   1. We characterize a region of incoherence and contraction for generalized latent factor models
      with missing data, thereby answering Question 1. It is further revealed that the gradient
      descent iterate remains in the region of incoherence and contraction and converge at a nearly
      linear rate to a statistically accurate stationary point, without explicit incoherence or balanc-
      ing regularization. Our guarantees accommodate severe missingness, weak low-rank signals,
      and diminishing local curvature of the link. We show that the resulting aggregate estimation
      rate matches the restricted-class minimax lower bound up to logarithmic factors.

   2. To warm-start gradient descent, we analyze a nonlinear spectral procedure that incorporates
      knowledge of the link function. We establish both a Frobenius-norm recovery guarantee and
      leave-one-out error control, yielding globally consistent estimators of ζ ∗ , X∗ , and Y∗ . Building
      on this spectral estimator, we show that a unilateral refinement step upgrades Frobenius-norm
      consistency to entrywise ℓ2,∞ consistency. These guarantees place the refined estimator within
      the region of incoherence and contraction, thereby providing the computationally tractable
      initialization sought in Question 2.

   3. We establish rowwise linear approximations for the output of gradient descent in nonlinear
      latent factor models with missing observations. These expansions isolate the leading score
      fluctuations from higher-order estimation and optimization errors, thereby making the output
  1       bn and θ ∗ generically represent estimated and population latent parameters, including intercepts and
    Here, θ
factors.

       of a nonconvex algorithm amenable to statistical inference. They yield asymptotically valid
       individual inference, with limiting covariance attaining the corresponding oracle Cramér–
       Rao lower bounds, without requiring an additional debiasing step. Building on the same
       expansions, we develop a Gaussian multiplier bootstrap for simultaneous inference on latent
       factors and missing entries with theoretical guarantees.

    4. On the technical side, we develop a leave-one-out analysis spanning the entire algorithmic
       pipeline, which yields fine-grained control of the iterative estimators despite the dependencies
       induced by missing observations. Building on this control, we establish the rowwise linear
       approximation through a delicate analysis of the global Hessian structure, complemented by
       an auxiliary-sequence argument.

Figure 1 provides a schematic summary of the three-stage computational pipeline, their correspond-
ing theoretical guarantees, and downstream statistical inference results.

Paper organization. The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section 2 presents
the main estimation and linear approximation results for warm-started gradient descent, together
with a discussion of the underlying assumptions. Section 3 describes the prior two-stage algorithmic
design for warm start. Section 4 develops the downstream statistical inference procedures. Section 6
provides numerical experiments that illustrate the practical performance of our methods. Section 7
applies our methods to evaluate large language models with uncertainty quantification. Section 8
concludes with a discussion of future directions. All proofs are deferred to the Supplementary
Material.


2     Warm-Started Gradient Descent: Estimation and Linear Ap-
      proximations
As described in the diagram (Figure 1), the overall procedure consists of three stages, each serving
a distinct role. For clarity, however, we present the algorithmic analyses in an order reverse to
that of the algorithm itself. We first present the estimation guarantees and linear approximation
properties for the gradient descent algorithm, from which we develop insights into the region of
incoherence and contraction needed for the warm start. Then, we turn to the earlier stages of

                                                     5
the pipeline that lead to the warm start. Notably, the output of each stage enjoys a progressively
stronger form of consistency, ultimately meeting the requirements for initializing gradient descent.

2.1    Notation
For any positive integer q, let [q] := {1, . . . , q}. We use 1q , 0q , and Iq for the all-ones vector, the zero
vector, and the identity matrix of dimension q, respectively. For A ∈ Rq1 ×q2 , Ai,· ∈ R1×q2 denotes
its ith row in row-vector form, whereas Ai := A⊤         i,· ∈ R
                                                                 q2 denotes the same row in column-vector

form; similarly, A·,j denotes its jth column. For a vector a, ∥a∥2 denotes its Euclidean norm.
For a matrix A, we write ∥A∥, ∥A∥F , and ∥A∥2,∞ := maxi∈[q1 ] ∥Ai ∥2 for its spectral, Frobenius,
and maximum rowwise Euclidean norms, respectively, and denote its kth largest singular value by
σk (A). The symbols (·)⊤ and ◦ represent the transpose and Hadamard product. For Ω ⊆ [n] × [p],
the sampling operator PΩ is defined by [PΩ (A)]ij := Aij 1{(i, j) ∈ Ω}, or equivalently PΩ (A) =
Ω◦A, where Ω is the associated binary mask. The operator Pa∞ denotes coordinatewise clipping to
[−a, a]. The map Pa2,∞ (A) rescales each row of A whose ℓ2 norm exceeds a so that its norm equals
a. For θ = [ζ, X, Y], define M(θ) := 1n ζ ⊤ + XY⊤ . We write O(r) := {O ∈ Rr×r : O⊤ O = Ir } for
the orthogonal group, and set d := n ∨ p and ρ := (n ∨ p)/(n ∧ p). Finally, an ≲ bn means an ≤ Cbn
for some constant C > 0, an ≍ bn means that both an ≲ bn and bn ≲ an , and an ≪ bn means that
there exists a sufficiently small c > 0 such that an ≤ cbn .

2.2    Model Assumptions
We first introduce the basic model setup, which accommodates a wide class of continuous and
discrete distributions. Let U∗ Σ∗ V∗⊤ denote the rank-r SVD of X∗ Y∗ ⊤ , with singular values
σ1∗ ≥ · · · ≥ σr∗ , and define κ := σ1∗ /σr∗ . We introduce the incoherence degree µ of the singular
vectors as follows:

Definition 1. The parameters [ζ ∗ , X∗ , Y∗ ] are said to be µ-incoherent if nr ∥U∗ ∥22,∞ ∨ pr ∥V∗ ∥22,∞ ≤
µ and ∥ζ ∗ − ζ0 · 1p ∥∞ ≤ µrσr∗ /np for some reference level ζ0 .

accounts for:
    • weak low-rank signal strength, with σr∗ approaching σ̄ d/π/cψ up to logarithmic factors;
                                                             p

   • severe missingness, with π as small as (n ∧ p)−1 up to logarithmic factors;

   • weak local curvature of the link, with cψ = o(1).
These regimes may overlap, and together they capture settings in which both statistical recovery and
algorithmic stability are delicate.

Identifiability conditions. Before proceeding, we note that the parameters in the generalized
latent factor model are not uniquely identifiable without further conditions, just as their linear
factor model counterparts. To disentangle the intercepts from the latent factors, we treat ζ ∗ as the
average response level and impose the following assumptions on the intercept and the factors:
Assumption 4 (Basic Identifiability). The factors X∗ , Y∗ and the intercept vector ζ ∗ obey:

      Following the discussions in Bai and Li (2012) and Fan et al. (2021), the parameter triple
[ζ ∗ , X∗ , Y∗ ] is identifiable only up to an orthogonal rotation; that is, (X∗ , Y∗ ) and (X∗ R, Y∗ R)
produce the same distribution for any R ∈ O(r). In particular, the first condition in (4) centers the
columns of X∗ , which renders ζ ∗ identifiable. The second condition in (5) fixes the relative scaling
between X∗ and Y∗ . The scaling factor ω > 0 is set a priori to accommodate Bayesian settings
in which the latent factors naturally operate on different scales. For instance, if the entries of X∗
and Y∗ are independently drawn from their respective priors, then one typically has ω ≍ n/p since
              √                      √
σk (X∗ ) ≍ n and σk (Y∗ ) ≍ p for k ∈ [r]. One may further strengthen the identifiability by
removing the rotational ambiguity, as we shall elaborate in Section 2.3.

2.3    Gradient Descent and Theoretical Guarantees
As all preceding steps (cf. Algorithms 2 and 3) are designed to guarantee the success of gradient
descent, we begin by identifying the initialization conditions it requires and the consequences that
follow. For a centering parameter c⊥ > 0, consider

    Regularization Choice. We briefly explain why the objective contains neither an incoherence
penalty nor a balancing penalty. As noted in the introduction, rowwise consistency prevents the
iterates from entering the unfavorable parts of the landscape created by missingness. One could


                                                         8
enforce this property by adding ∥X∥22,∞ + ∥Y∥22,∞ , but such a penalty would complicate the opti-
mization. Inspired by Ma et al. (2018), we instead prove that vanilla gradient descent enjoys implicit
regularization, in that it implicitly preserves rowwise consistency when initialized from a rowwise-
consistent estimate. Prior work also commonly adds the balancing penalty X⊤ X − Y⊤ Y F when
ω = 1 to control the relative scales of the two factors. Algorithm 1 imposes balance only once,
through an exact refactorization of the warm start. No balancing penalty is used thereafter. We
show that the scaled learning rates preserve approximate balance along the ensuing trajectory.
Section 2.4 describes these two forms of implicit regularization in detail.

   We are now positioned to present our estimation error control for the gradient descent iterates.

gives the geometric decay and the stochastic-error floor in Theorem 2.1; the proof also establishes
the corresponding rowwise recursion.
Remark 6. In line with the implicit-regularization phenomenon identified by Ma et al. (2018), our
analysis permits a learning rate close to the most aggressive rate allowed by population gradient
descent. Specifically, Theorem 2.1 permits η ≍ (c⊥ πσ1∗ )−1 , whereas the largest stable learning rate
for the population negative log-likelihood is of order (σ 2 πσ1∗ )−1 . When σ is also of constant order,
the two rates differ only by a polylogarithmic factor in d.
    More importantly, beyond the estimation error control described above, we provide fine-grained
linear approximations by quantifying their residual errors from the gradient descent iterates.

   We highlight the following implications.

   • Sample complexity. When the remaining structural quantities are bounded, our assumptions
     require π(n ∧ p) to grow only polylogarithmically in d. Thus, the sampling requirement lies
     within logarithmic factors of the 1/(n ∧ p) degrees-of-freedom scale familiar from fixed-rank
     matrix completion (Candès and Tao, 2009; Candes and Recht, 2009). In this low-sampling
     regime, missingness destroys local ℓ2 convexity outside the region analyzed in Section 2.4.

   • Sparse scenarios. Consider the Bernoulli model with the logistic link in the balanced regime
     n ≍ p, and write qn := ψ(ζ0 ). When µ is fixed, σr∗ ≍ σ1∗ ≍ n, and the factor contribution is
     uniformly bounded, all success probabilities are of order qn . Under the sparse-link regularity
                      1/2
     condition, σ ≍ qn , cψ ≍ σ 2 ≍ qn , Lψ ≍ 1. The fixed-rank approximation factor contributes
     only a polylogarithmic term. The threshold and approximation conditions are then satisfied
     if πnqn ≫ (log n)5r+4 . Under full observation model without missing data, the estimation
     and rowwise linearization theory therefore permits qn = n−1 (log n)c for any fixed c > 5r + 4.
     This response regime is strictly sparser than the qn ≍ n−1+ϵ regime considered by Li et al.
     (2023) and Cui and Xu (2026), whereas Ouyang et al. (2024) assumes curvature bounded
     away from zero and therefore excludes vanishing Bernoulli probabilities. Polylogarithmic
     expected degrees have also been accommodated for Frobenius-norm estimation by Ma et al.
     (2020) and for spectral inference under a linear random-dot-product link by Rubin-Delanchy
     et al. (2022). The distinction of our result is the combination of a nonlinear generalized latent
     factor model, missing observations, uniform rowwise linearization, and inferential guarantees.

   • No need for debiasing. The linear approximation is readily seen to be mean zero. In contrast
     to convex programming approaches (Chen et al., 2019b) for the linear matrix completion, our
     method does not require an additional debiasing step, since the underlying optimization
     procedure avoids bias-inducing regularization.

2.4     Proof architecture for the gradient stage
Several highlights of our technical contributions of the gradient descent analysis are in order. We
begin by characterizing the optimization landscape, and then discuss the key technical ingredients
underlying the linear approximations.

2.4.1     Local curvature and implicit regularization
We now shed light on the behavior of gradient descent iterates and on why a warm start is necessary.
As a point of reference, consider the linear setting of low-rank matrix completion. The seminal
work Ma et al. (2018) offered a new perspective through the lens of implicit regularization, showing
that gradient descent can automatically constrain its trajectory to remain within the region of
incoherence and contraction (RIC). This property makes it possible to obtain precise control of
the Hessian along tangent directions at any point in the RIC. In this paper, we show that this
implicit regularization phenomenon extends to generalized latent factor models with missingness.
Moreover, in our analysis, implicit regularization manifests itself in two distinct senses: (i) locational
regularization within the RIC, and (ii) approximate balancedness between X∗ and Y∗ .

   • Implicit confinement to the RIC. In a nutshell, the region of incoherence and contraction
     (RIC) comprises parameters that are close to [ζ ∗ , X∗ , Y∗ ] in both global and rowwise norms.
     As it turns out, restricted strong convexity and smoothness continue to hold after augmenting
     the empirical loss with a balancing penalty. Specifically, define
                                                                             2
                                 Laug (θ) = L(θ) + caug π X⊤ X − ωY⊤ Y F .                           (11)

        with caug ≍ cψ . The curvature of Laug , along directions that approximately lie in the tangent
        space at [ζ, X, Y], admits upper and lower bounds of nearly the same order as those of
        its population counterpart, obtained by taking expectation with respect to both R and Ω.
        Meanwhile, the behavior of the actual iterates hinges on the curvature of Laug , enabling us to
        obtain near linear convergence rates (cf. Remark 5). However, this invariance of the trajectory
        does not follow from local curvature condition alone; it depends on finer structural properties
        of the loss and the factorized parametrization. We build upon the implicit-regularization
        perspective of Ma et al. (2018), Chen et al. (2019a), Chen et al. (2021c) , and Fan et al.


                                                   12
        (2025a) and show that, when suitably initialized, vanilla gradient descent remains in the
        RIC, without an explicit incoherence or balancing penalty.
        We emphasize that our implicit regularization analysis relies crucially on a leave-one-out
        argument, which decouples the dependence between the iterate [ζ t , Xt , Yt ] and a fixed row
        or column of PΩ (R − ψ(M∗ )). Because gradient descent is initialized by the first two stages,
        the coupled construction must begin at the spectral estimator and continue through one-step
        refinement; see Section 3.

   • Implicit balancedness.    We also prove that throughout the iterates, the gradient descent
     algorithm is able to maintain the balancedness conditions appearing in the identifiability
     condition (4), without explicitly imposing regularization such as X⊤ X − ωY⊤ Y F . This
     form of implicit regularization is noteworthy for two reasons. First, unlike Tu et al. (2016),
     Zheng and Lafferty (2016), and Park et al. (2018), we do not impose any explicit regularization
     term to guarantee them. Second, they resonate with the discussion in Soltanolkotabi et al.
     (2023) for matrix sensing, but here it arises in a subtler notion of balancedness that is essential
     for the downstream inferential procedures.

2.4.2     Key Ingredients of Linear Approximations
Having established that the gradient descent iterates provably surf through a nice landscape, we
now elaborate on how the linear approximation in Theorem 2.2 is derived from the final gradient
descent iterate. This derivation constitutes the main technical contribution of our gradient descent
analysis.
        bt0 denote the concatenation of [ζ t0 , Xt0 , Yt0 ]. At a high level, for a convex objective, the
    Let θ
desired linear approximation typically follows from two ingredients: (i) the gradient at the current
iterate is sufficiently small in the rowwise sense, and (ii) the estimation error admits a heuristic
representation like (3).
    A critical complication, however, is that, leaving rotational ambiguity alone, the actual objective
function L in (6), unlike the augmented one in Section 2.4.1, is nonconvex and fails to be strongly
convex in all directions, even locally. This degeneracy stems from the factorized parametrization:
the latent factors are invariant under simultaneous rescalings, rendering the Hessian matrix sin-
gular. Consequently, controlling the gradient norm rowwisely becomes challenging, for instance,
  ∇X L(θ t0 ) 2,∞ , particularly when the iteration time t0 is chosen to be rather small, while the
singularity of the Hessian also rules out directly applying the inverse appearing in (3).
    To address these challenges, we combine three complementary technical ingredients in the the-
oretical analysis, summarized below.

   • Rowwise score decomposition. Our analysis is guided by the following decomposition. For

3      Constructing Good Starts for the Gradient Descent
As noted earlier, a fundamental prerequisite for analyzing the gradient-descent trajectory is that
the initializer lies within the region of incoherence and contraction. For models with a low-rank
mean structure, a spectral initializer constructed from the observed matrix PΩ (R) is often suffi-
cient (Ma et al., 2018), owing to recent advances in entrywise eigenspace and singular-subspace
perturbation theory (Abbe et al., 2020; Agterberg et al., 2022; Cai et al., 2021). In the generalized
model, however, directly applying a truncated SVD to the observation matrix, whose expectation
is πψ 1n ζ ∗ ⊤ + X∗ Y∗ ⊤ , typically yields little direct information about the latent factors. The
entrywise nonlinear transformation ψ distorts the underlying low-rank structure and, in general,
does not preserve the rank of the latent signal matrix.
    Nonetheless, we resolve this mismatch through a two-step SVD procedure, similar to the spirit
of Ma et al. (2020) and Zhang et al. (2020). For this initial double-SVD stage, we develop a new
analysis tailored to the present model and establish Frobenius-norm consistency and leave-one-out
stability of the resulting factor estimates. The remaining challenge is then to upgrade such global
consistency to row-wise consistency, as required for the initializer to enter the region of incoherence
and contraction introduced in Section 2.4. To this end, we incorporate a one-step refinement
procedure, inspired by Chen and Li (2024), to sharpen the spectral initializer.

3.1     First Step: Double SVD with Link Inversion
The first step of the optimization pipeline is to construct an initializer that is sufficiently close
to the true parameters under a suitable metric. Specifically, we first apply an SVD to the zero-
filled observation matrix PΩ (R), then apply ψ −1 entrywise to the resulting estimate (e.g., ψ −1
is the logit function in the Bernoulli case), and finally perform a second rank-r truncated SVD,
following the general spirit of Ma et al. (2020); Zhang et al. (2020). To facilitate the leave-one-out
analysis, we replace universal singular value thresholding (Chatterjee, 2015) with soft singular value
thresholding (Koltchinskii et al., 2011). The complete procedure is presented in Algorithm 2.
     While the spectrum of πψ(1n ζ ∗⊤ + X∗ Y∗⊤ ) is not fully understood, this approach does not

                                                          15
require the transformed mean matrix to be rank-r. The threshold specified below balances the nor-
malized global estimation error against the leave-one-out discrepancy. √   Under the stated sampling
and signal-strength conditions, it also dominates the base noise scale σ̄ πd and the nonlinear ap-
proximation error. This feature is particularly useful for nonlinear transformations, which generally
inflate the rank of the mean matrix.
    Formally, we have the following theorem, whose proof is deferred to Section B.

Theorem 3.1 (Spectral Initialization). Suppose that Assumptions 1, 2, 3, and 4 hold. Choose

3.2    Second Step: Unilateral Refinement
The unilateral refinement (UR) procedure in Algorithm 3 first clip the spectral estimates to control
their ℓ∞ or ℓ2,∞ norm. It then performs two independent likelihood refinements: one estimates X
with (ζ 0 , Y0 ) fixed, while the other estimates (ζ, Y) with X0 fixed. Both subproblems decompose
into low-dimensional convex programs and can be solved efficiently using first- or second-order
methods.
    The intuition behind this refinement is that, when estimating the ith row of the latent factor
matrix, the optimizer X   b ur isolates the influence of the ith-row noise from that of the remaining
                            i
rows, thereby enabling sharper row-specific error control. This improvement is further corroborated
by the numerical experiments in Section 6. Formally, we have the following theorem whose proof
is presented in Section C.

    The three terms in the error bound arise, respectively, from the intrinsic refinement noise,
the balanced contribution of the global and leave-one-out errors from the spectral stage, and the
nonlinear missingness remainder. Thus, the refinement turns the spectral initializer’s global con-
sistency and stability into uniform rowwise control. This, in turn, allows us to identify a rather
mild condition under which the output of the first two steps enters the region of incoherence and
contraction.

Remark 9. Chen and Li (2024) analyze one-step refinements with and without data splitting.
Their bound for the no-splitting case contains an additional π −1/2 factor, although this procedure
performs better in their simulations. In our setting, the analogous difficulty is the dependence in
                        b sp . We control this dependence by leave-one-out coupling throughout the
terms such as PΩ (E)i,· Y
pipeline, without further sample splitting.

   Together with the centering-and-balancing step in Algorithm 1, these guarantees furnish the
warm start needed for Theorem 2.1. Under the additional conditions of Theorem 2.2, the resulting
gradient-descent output admits rowwise linear expansions, which underpin the inference procedures
developed in the next section.


4      Individual and Simultaneous Uncertainty Quantification
The linear expansions in Theorem 2.2 reduce inference to the distribution of rowwise score sums.
We shall start from individual inference, as a direct consequence of asymptotic normality. Going
beyond, we extend the scenario to the simultaneous inference, leveraging the Gaussian multiplier
bootstrap technique.       To streamline the notation,    we introduce the appended factor estimates

Remark 10. To assess the optimality of our inference procedure, we observe that the inverses
of the normalizing terms match the Cramér–Rao lower bounds for estimating an individual entry
or row of ζ ∗ , X∗ , or Y∗ , under the oracle setting in which all remaining parameters are known.
Formally, suppose that we have access to the intercept ζ ∗ as well as the right factor Y∗ . Then for
any unbiased estimator x b for X∗i , we have
                                       x | Ω) ⪰ H∗−1

                                                 Xi = CRLB(Xi | Ω).

This lower bound coincides with the limiting covariance in Theorem 4.1, thereby establishing oracle
efficiency. The same argument applies to ζ ∗ and Y∗ .

4.2   Simultaneous Confidence Bands via Gaussian Multiplier Bootstrap
The preceding results quantify uncertainty for a fixed number of parameters. Many applications,
however, require simultaneous comparisons over a collection whose size may grow with the di-
mensions. Fortunately, our new uniform rowwise linear approximations results make it possible
to approximate the distributions of the resulting maximum statistics by a Gaussian multiplier
bootstrap.
   We begin with a unified framework covering a broad class of maxima over linear functionals of
the latent factors. Specifically, consider targets of the form

    The validity of this procedure follows from combining our rowwise linear approximations with
high-dimensional central limit theorems (Chernozhukov et al., 2013, 2022, 2023a). Its score-
perturbation construction is related to existing bootstrap approaches for simultaneous inference
(Belloni et al., 2018; Zhang and Cheng, 2017; Chernozhukov et al., 2013). To our knowledge, our
result is among the first to justify simultaneous inference for the intercepts and both latent factor
matrices in generalized latent factor models with missing observations. A central difficulty is that

                                                             19
both factor matrices are unknown: the score for X, for example, depends on the estimated Y. Es-
tablishing bootstrap validity therefore requires controlling how estimation errors in the two factors
propagate through the score statistics.

    In words, provided that the rotation and weight estimation errors are sufficiently small, the
multiplier bootstrap yields asymptotically valid simultaneous inference for a broad class of maxi-
mum statistics over L functionals, where L may grow polynomially with d. The requisite control
of the rotation error may follow from Proposition 3 of the Supplementary Material.
    We next consider two applications of simultaneous inference.

Ranking intervals for factor entries. Factor ranking is central to LLM evaluation, with public
leaderboards ordering models by aggregate benchmark scores or pairwise human preferences (Chi-
ang et al., 2024; Alzahrani et al., 2024). Such rankings can be sensitive to minor evaluation choices
(Alzahrani et al., 2024). Our latent factors instead yield capability-specific rankings of models and
questions; since each rank is determined by pairwise differences, Theorem 4.3 yields simultaneous
confidence intervals for these ranks.

                                                                  21
5     Related Literature
The literature on latent factor models is vast; see Bartholomew et al. (2008) for an overview.
Low-rank estimation under linear observation models is studied by, among others, Candes and
Recht (2009); Chen et al. (2019a, 2020a, 2019b). Nonlinear observation models and one-bit matrix
completion are considered in Davenport et al. (2014); Cai and Zhou (2013); Chen and Li (2024).
    Inference for linear low-rank models, including incomplete panels, has been developed in Chen
et al. (2019b); Xie and Xu (2023); Chernozhukov et al. (2023b); Xiong and Pelger (2023); Choi et al.
(2024); Su and Wang (2025). Related likelihood-based theory for nonlinear panels, comparison,
and networks is studied in Wang (2022); Chen et al. (2021a); Gao et al. (2023); Li et al. (2023);
Chen et al. (2023); Ouyang et al. (2024); Tian et al. (2026); Li et al. (2026); Gao et al. (2022). By
contrast, we characterize the actual output of a computationally tractable, warm-started nonconvex
pipeline under severe missingness and derive uniform rowwise linear approximations that support
simultaneous inference for the intercepts, latent factors, and missing-entry means.
    Our optimization dynamics analysis is related to the implicit-regularization literature for linear
latent factor models (Chen et al., 2020a, 2019b; Ma et al., 2018) and nonlinear low-rank estimation
(Cui and Xu, 2026). Beyond rowwise confinement, we quantify the implicit balancedness of the
factor iterates, a phenomenon related to Du et al. (2018); Ma et al. (2021); Soltanolkotabi et al.
(2023), with refined bounds tailored to downstream statistical inference.


6     Simulation Studies
This section evaluates the empirical performance of the proposed computational pipeline and sta-
tistical inference methods. We first examine the estimation errors of the final gradient descent
estimator (Algorithm 1) under varying signal strength and sampling rates, in comparison with the
preceding stages (Algorithms 2 and 3). Then, we conduct experiments to verify the inferential
guarantees provided in Section 4.
    Consider the logistic latent factor model

                 Rij | Mij∗ ∼ Bernoulli 1/(1 + exp(−Mij∗ )) ,   ∗
                                                                   = ζj∗ + X∗⊤  ∗
                                                           
                                                              Mi,j          i Yj ,

where each√entry is observedpindependently with probability π. The left latent factor is gener-
ated from λn · ŪEU · diag( 3/2, 1), where Ū ∈ O(n, n − 1) is an arbitrary orthonormal matrix
with    ⊤      and EU is sampled from Unif(Stiefel(r, n − 1)), while the right latent factor follows
√ 1n Ū = 0 p
  λp·EV ·diag( 3/2, 1) with EV ∼ Unif(Stiefel(r, p)). The resulting matrix X∗ Y∗ ⊤ is then rescaled
through the top-r singular value decomposition so that the identifiability constraints with ω = 1 are
satisfied. We sample each entry of ζ ∗ independently from Unif([−0.1, 0.1]) and use π = 0.5 as the
baseline sampling rate. The factor learning rate is η = 0.5/(πσ1∗ ). In the numerical implementation,
we omit the input trimming in Algorithm 3 and apply no clipping during unilateral refinement.
Throughout, we align the two factor estimates at each stage to (X∗ , Y∗ ) using their common or-
thogonal Procrustes rotation, defined as in Theorem 2.1; the rotation for the final iterate is denoted
by Ot0 . We find that the algorithm is relatively insensitive to the choice of hyperparameters; the
remaining implementation details are reported in Section G of the Supplementary Material.

6.1   Experiments with Various Signal Strength and Missing Levels
We begin with the full estimator. The goal of this experiment is to identify the practical stability
region of the pipeline after all three stages have been applied. We set n = 1000, p = 500, and r = 2,


                                                 22
Figure 2: Estimation errors across algorithmic stages under varying signal strength λ. Here, the
relative Frobenius error is defined as Xt0 Ot0 − X∗ F / ∥X∗ ∥F , whereas the relative ℓ2,∞ error is
 Xt0 Ot0 − X∗ 2,∞ / ∥X∗ ∥2,∞ . The corresponding errors for Yt0 are defined analogously.




Figure 3: Estimation errors across algorithmic stages under varying sampling rate π; the errors are
calculated using the same convention as Figure 2.


and vary the signal strength λ ∈ {0.5, 0.6, · · · , 1.0} and the sampling rate π ∈ {0.2, 0.4, · · · , 1.0}.
Figures 2 and 3 plot the mean relative errors against the signal strength λ and the sampling rate
π, with separate panels for π and separate curves for λ.
    Across 200 Monte Carlo replications, the relative Frobenius errors generally decrease as either
the signal strength λ or the observation probability π increases. The spectral estimator provides an
informative initialization, the one-step refinement substantially improves rowwise accuracy, and the
final gradient-descent stage typically produces a further reduction in estimation error. These gains
become particularly pronounced as π increases, indicating that denser observations improve the
stability of both model- and task-side factor recovery.2 Overall, the results demonstrate the benefits
of the proposed multistage procedure and show that its performance improves systematically under
stronger signals and more complete observation.

We first conduct numerical experiments to verify the asymptotic normality of the entries as well as
the consistency of the bootstrap procedures. We start with the asymptotic normality of (X∗ )1,1 , as
predicted by Theorem 4.2. We then turn to the distributions of the maximum statistic maxi∈[n] (Xb t0 −

    Next, we consider two simultaneous confidence-band construction tasks based on the Gaus-
sian multiplier bootstrap procedures developed in Section 4: confidence intervals for the rankings
constructed from the pairwise maximum statistic (cf. (17), (18), and (19)) and studentized predic-
tion intervals for the missing entries (cf. (20)). Table 1 reports their empirical coverage rates and
average interval widths at the nominal 95% level. The rank intervals attain 100% simultaneous
coverage in all settings, although their widths reflect some conservativeness. This is a familiar cost
of simultaneously inverting a large collection of pairwise comparisons (Bhattacharya et al., 2026;
Fan et al., 2025b). The prediction bands exceed the nominal level except in the sparsest setting,
π = 0.1.


7    Real Data Application to LLM Evaluation
We analyze the binary model–question response matrix constructed from the primary split of the
Metabench dataset (Kipnis et al., 2025). The rows of the data matrix are indexed by the LLMs
and the columns are indexed by the questions, while the binary value indicates if the correspond-
ing model answers the question correctly. We fit the data by the Bernoulli generalized latent
factor model (2). Metabench aggregates item-wise evaluation records from the Open LLM Leader-
board and selects a compact set of diagnostic questions across ARC, GSM8K, HellaSwag, MMLU,
TruthfulQA, and WinoGrande. For the present analysis we use the 858 primary Metabench items,
consisting of 145 ARC questions, 237 GSM8K questions, 93 HellaSwag questions, 96 MMLU ques-
tions, 154 TruthfulQA questions, and 133 WinoGrande questions. The processed response matrix
contains 5,055 models with complete responses on all 858 items. We set the factor number to r = 3,
under which the estimated structure remains stable and interpretable across random data splits,
and apply the Varimax rotation described in Section 2.3.
    We begin by examining the estimated right factors, which characterize variation across ques-

tions. Figure 5a displays their pairwise relationships, while Figure 5b reveals a pronounced block-
sparse structure across benchmark sources. Questions from GSM8K load most strongly on the first
factor; because these questions emphasize multistep arithmetic and mathematical problem solv-
ing, we call this factor quantitative reasoning. The second factor is driven primarily by HellaSwag
and WinoGrande, which require models to apply commonsense knowledge to identify plausible
events and resolve contextual relationships, motivating the label procedural commonsense. Finally,
TruthfulQA loads most strongly on the third factor; because this benchmark tests whether models
resist common misconceptions and produce truthful responses, we call this factor misconception
resistance. Together, these loading patterns provide a coherent substantive interpretation of the
estimated latent structure.
    Figure 6 reports pointwise and simultaneous confidence intervals, together with simultaneous
rank confidence intervals, for the question-level estimates, following the procedures developed in
Sections 4.1 and 4.2. The benchmark composition of the highest-ranked questions reinforces the
factor interpretations: questions from GSM8K, HellaSwag, and TruthfulQA are prominent under
the quantitative-reasoning, procedural-commonsense, and misconception-resistance factors, respec-
tively. The accompanying rank intervals quantify the uncertainty in these orderings rather than
treating the estimated rankings as exact. In addition, the question-intercept estimate ζ t0 closely
tracks the corresponding average accuracies, supporting their interpretation as baseline easiness,
or equivalently as negative difficulty under the present sign convention. Turning to the model-level
results, Figure 7 displays confidence intervals and simultaneous rank intervals for the five models
with the largest estimated scores along each latent capability. Although the point estimates suggest
an ordering, every displayed simultaneous rank interval includes rank one, and many span dozens
or even hundreds of positions. The data therefore do not support a decisive ranking among the
leading models, cautioning against treating small differences in aggregate benchmark performance
as conclusive evidence of model superiority.

8    Conclusion and Discussion
We have introduced and analyzed a three-step algorithm for uncertainty quantification in general-
ized latent factor models with missing data. First, we obtain a Frobenius-consistent initialization
via a double-SVD procedure. Next, we refine these estimates through a unilateral refinement pro-
cedure to achieve rowwise consistency. Finally, we apply vanilla gradient descent, benefiting from
its implicit regularization, to locate an approximate stationary point of the nonconvex likelihood.
     Our theoretical and empirical results confirm that this workflow delivers both accurate point
estimates and valid confidence bands. The linear approximation of the gradient-descent iterates
underpins our inferential framework, allowing us to characterize their limiting distributions and
to construct individual and simultaneous confidence sets via the Gaussian multiplier bootstrap.
Moreover, by analyzing the loss landscape within the region of incoherence and contraction, we
show why gradient descent remains robust to local minima and permits optimal estimation errors.
The optimality manifests in three complementary senses. First, the aggregate error matches the
restricted-class minimax lower bound in Theorem 2.3 up to logarithmic factors and the limiting
variance coincides with the corresponding Cramér-Rao lower bound. Second, for balanced logistic
Bernoulli models, where the scale and sparsity of the average success probability is of interest, the
theory requires only πnψ(ζ0 ) ≫ (log n)5r+4 ; under full observation, it therefore permits success
probabilities of order n−1 (log n)c for any fixed c > 5r + 4. Third, the sampling rate π attains the
information-theoretic lower bound up to logarithmic factors.
     Our simulations demonstrate that the proposed method achieves high estimation precision and
reliable coverage. This work thus provides a practical and theoretically grounded toolkit for infer-
ence in high-dimensional latent factor settings with missingness. Its application to the Metabench
dataset validates its effectiveness in extracting hidden factors from LLM responses and in uncer-
tainty quantification of their latent capabilities.
     Several paths are left open for further work: (i) such nonlinearity-encoded factor structure is
also natural for symmetric network data as well as tensor data, thus it would be interesting to
see whether we can have computationally tractable and statistically optimal procedures for such
models; (ii) While our analysis reaches the fixed-rank degrees-of-freedom sampling scale up to
logarithmic factors in the balanced regime (n ≈ p), the singular subspace perturbation theory for
the linear models (Cai and Zhang, 2018; Zhang et al., 2022; Cai et al., 2021) suggests that, in the


                                                 26
Figure 6: Ranking uncertainty for the rotated question factors and question intercepts. Each panel
highlights the three highest-ranked, three middle-ranked, and three lowest-ranked questions, with

Questions between these groups are displayed in compressed rank order: blue and orange shaded
bands represent their simultaneous and pointwise confidence intervals, while the orange curve traces
their point estimates. Parenthetical values report simultaneous rank confidence intervals; labels in
the intercept panel additionally report each question’s average accuracy.

unbalanced regime (n ≫ p or n ≪ p), the error rates for the left and right factors should scale
according to their respective dimensions. Characterizing the sharp dependence on these dimensions
in the present nonlinear setting is an important question for future study.

A      Preliminary Setups

Roadmap of the Supplementary Material
The theoretical analysis in this Supplementary Material follows the computational pipeline, from
initialization to statistical inference. Section A records the parameter-explicit assumptions and
notation, allowing the parameters treated as constants in the main text to vary.
    Section B analyzes the double-SVD initializer. We first establish global estimation accuracy by
tracking the errors through singular value thresholding, entrywise link inversion, and the second low-
rank factorization. We then construct leave-one-row-out and leave-one-column-out counterparts
and bound their discrepancies from the original estimates. These stability bounds provide the
decoupling needed for the subsequent refinement analysis.
    Section C analyzes the two unilateral likelihood refinements, each holding the preliminary es-
timates on the opposite side fixed. Leave-one-out decoupling and control of the sampled Hessians
yield rowwise error bounds despite the dependence between the preliminary estimates and the ob-
servations. We further establish leave-one-out stability of the refined estimates. Together, these
results supply the accuracy and stability requirements for the warm-started gradient-descent anal-
ysis, without additional sample splitting.
    Section D presents the parameter-explicit gradient-descent and linear-approximation guaran-
tees, whose proofs proceed through three dependent steps:

    1. Control of the gradient-descent trajectory. We establish the local geometry and use leave-one-
       out induction to control estimation errors, incoherence, centering, and balancedness under a
       general warm start. Verifying that the first two stages supply the required initialization then
       yields the gradient-descent guarantee for the full pipeline.

    2. From trajectory control to linear approximation. An auxiliary sequence under the augmented
       objective provides rowwise approximate stationarity. We then control the weighted nuisance
       terms through a global error decomposition that accounts for the nonidentifiable directions.
       These ingredients, combined with a rowwise score expansion, yield the linear representations
       in Section D.7.

    3. From linear approximation to inference. Section D.8 combines these representations with
       Gaussian and multiplier-bootstrap approximations to establish the individual and simultane-
       ous inferential guarantees and their downstream applications. Section D.9 studies data-driven
       recovery of the rotations needed for interpretable factor inference.

    Section E establishes the minimax lower bound. The remaining sections collect auxiliary results
on sampling, concentration, matrix perturbation, and distributional approximation, followed by the
numerical implementation details in Section G.

A.1     Parameter-Explicit Assumptions
For completeness, we record the parameter-explicit assumptions used throughout the supplementary
analysis. These conditions allow r, κ, and κψ to vary, and reduce to Assumptions 1–4 under the
fixed-parameter regime adopted in the main text.

Scaling relation between X and Y. As discussed earlier, we introduce a prescribed scaling
parameter ω > 0 to accommodate settings in which the two latent factors operate on different
scales. Nevertheless, it is sufficient to restrict attention to the balanced case X∗ ⊤ X∗ = Y∗ ⊤ Y∗
without loss of generality, as shown by the following lemma.
Lemma 1. Let the superscript ω denote the output of the algorithm under scaling parameter ω.
                                           (ω)        (1)    (ω)         (1)
Suppose that the refinement radii satisfy ιX = ω 1/4 ιX and ιY = ω −1/4 ιY , and that the gradient
                                            (ω)   (1)                           (ω)     (1)  (ω)
stage uses the same centering parameter, c⊥ = c⊥ , with step sizes satisfying ηζ = ηζ , ηX =
     (1)          (ω)                  (1)
ω 1/2 ηX , and ηY = ω −1/2 ηY . Then the outputs of the algorithmic pipeline with scaling parameter
ω are related to those with ω = 1 through the following identities:

                                  b sp,ω ω − 14 = X
                                  X               b sp,1 ,     b sp,ω ω 14 = Y
                                                               Y             b sp,1 ,
                                  b ur,ω ω − 41 = X
                                  X               b ur,1 ,     b ur,ω ω 14 = Y
                                                               Y             b ur,1 ,
                                  b t,ω ω − 14 = X
                                  X              b t,1 ,     b t,ω ω 41 = Y
                                                             Y            b t,1 ,       ∀ t ∈ [t0 ].

Proof. The first two identities follow from a change of variables. The centering step commutes
with this rescaling, while the intercept estimate is left unchanged. The SVD refactorization then

                                                                38
introduces the factors ω 1/4 and ω −1/4 . The factor ω −1/2 in the centering penalty makes the entire
objective invariant under this rescaling. Consequently, the scaled gradient updates preserve the
relation inductively.

    As a result, the scaling parameter ω is assumed to be 1 throughout the remaining analysis.

Example of Parameters in Assumption 6.                      We illustrate Assumption 6 using the sparse
logistic link.

Lemma 2. Let ψ(x) = 1/(1 + e−x ) and Dψ = [ζ0 − b, ζ0 + b], where b = O(1) and ζ0 + b ≤ −1. For
every k ≥ 1,
                                 sup |ψ (k) (x)| ≤ Ceζ0 +b k!2k .
                                     x∈Dψ

Moreover, ψ ′ (x) ≍ ex uniformly on Dψ . Consequently, Assumption 6 holds with σ 2 ≍ eζ0 +b , Lψ ≍ 1,
cψ ≍ eζ0 −b , and κψ ≍ e2b .

Proof. For x ∈ Dψ and |z − x| = 1/2, one has Re(z) ≤ ζ0 + b + 1/2 ≤ −1/2. Hence |ψ(z)| =
|ez /(1 + ez )| ≤ Ceζ0 +b . Cauchy’s integral formula therefore gives

                           |ψ (k) (x)| ≤ k!2k     sup       |ψ(z)| ≤ Ceζ0 +b k!2k .
                                                |z−x|=1/2


The curvature claim follows from ψ ′ (x) = ex /(1 + ex )2 ≍ ex on the stated interval.

B.3.1    Proof of Lemma 3
We first remark on a useful byproduct of the preceding arguments that the number of singular
values of R∗ exceeding the threshold λsp is relatively small. Consequently, there must exist at least
one singular value of non-negligible magnitude, and there must also be a nontrivial gap among
these singular values. This observation suggests that the previous results are sufficient to support
a leave-one-out perturbation analysis developed in Abbe et al. (2020); Chen et al. (2021b).
    Specifically, we group the eigenvalues into blocks separated by nontrivial eigengaps, enabling
the application of established leave-one-out error control argument within each block. A potential
concern is that the soft-thresholding step partially truncates some singular values, which could
complicate the analysis. This issue is mitigated by observing that the soft-thresholding operator is
Lipschitz, so controlling  the unthresholded quantity is sufficient to derive the final bound.
          sp       PΩ (R) − πR∗ ≤ λsp /2 . By Lemma 23 and Weyl’s inequality, P(E sp ) ≥ 1 −

Controlling Matrix Estimation Errors. Finally, with the above singular subspace analysis in
place, we are able to establish the guarantee for the leave-one-out coupling. We first distinguish
scalar soft thresholding from its matrix-valued counterpart. For a matrix A = Pdiag(σl (A))Q⊤ ,
define
                               SVTs (A) := Pdiag (σl (A) − s)+ Q⊤ .
                                                               

This map is the proximal operator of s ∥·∥∗ and is therefore nonexpansive in the Frobenius norm.
Recall that an operator T is nonexpansive under the Frobenius norm if

                                  ∥T (A) − T (B)∥F ≤ ∥A − B∥F

                                                  49
for all conformable matrices A and B; see also Parikh and Boyd (2014, Sections 2.3 and 6.7.3).
Importantly, this is a property of the full matrix-valued SVT operator. It is also bi-orthogonally
equivariant: for arbitrary orthogonal matrices Q1 and Q2 ,

                                         SVTs (Q1 AQ⊤                 ⊤
                                                    2 ) = Q1 SVTs (A)Q2 .                                              (43)

In particular, when A is a nonnegative diagonal matrix, SVTs (A) = Ts (A).
    On E sp , zeroing the ith row cannot increase the spectral norm, and hence                            R(i) − πR∗     ≤

C     Unilateral Refinement Analysis
This section studies the unilateral refinement procedure (Algorithm 3) initialized by the spectral
estimator (Algorithm 2). The main message is that a single refinement step is enough to strengthen
a spectral/Frobenius-norm consistent estimator into a row-wise consistent one. Moreover, extending
the leave-one-out analysis developed for the spectral estimator, we establish a corresponding leave-
one-out analysis for the refinement step, which in turn prepares the ground for the decoupling
analysis of the final gradient descent stage.

C.1    A General Version of Theorem 3.2
We begin with a general formulation of the unilateral refinement algorithm by imposing conditions
on the initial estimators and their leave-one-out counterparts, without yet specifying their explicit
forms.

    We use the following probability bookkeeping throughout this proof. A concentration bound
for one fixed row or column is invoked with failure probability O(d−c−1 ), whereas a bound for one
fixed pair of row or column indices is invoked with failure probability O(d−c−2 ). Since n + p ≤ 2d
and np ≤ d2 , the corresponding uniform events have failure probability O(d−c ). The numerical
constants in the Bernstein thresholds are enlarged accordingly; this does not alter any displayed
rate because the thresholds already scale with log d. The truncation event in Assumption 5, whose
failure probability is O(d−c−10 ), is absorbed into all these events.
    Fix the constant cDψ in Assumption 6 sufficiently large relative to the constants in the centered
intercept and factor row bounds of Theorem C.1, including the fixed-point neighborhoods used be-
low. For each parameter triple in these neighborhoods, |M(θ)i,j − ζ0 | ≤ |ζj − ζ0 | + ∥Xi ∥2 ∥Yj ∥2 ≲
       √
µrσ1∗ / np. Thus the global and leave-one-out predictors, and the mixed predictors used in the uni-
lateral updates, belong to Dψ . Since this domain is an interval, the natural-parameter interpolation
segments used in the mean-value and Taylor expansions remain in the same domain.

C.3.1   Rowwise Error Control for Algorithm 3.
This part aims to establish (48). The core of our argument combines a fixed-point theorem with
the convexity of the loss function when one factor is held fixed, which establishes the existence of
a stationary point. In addition, the uniqueness of the solution follows from the strong convexity of
the objective function.

     Applying Brouwer’s fixed point theorem.        Equipped with the characterization of the terms

For each fixed pair (i, i′ ), all concentration inputs to (77) are invoked with failure probability
O(d−c−2 ). A union bound over (i, i′ ) ∈ [n]2 therefore makes (76) valid uniformly with failure
probability O(d−c ).
The leave-one-column-out Hessian is handled similarly. Fix any i′ ∈ [n] and j ∈ [p]. Its
kth summand is weighted by 1{Ωi′ ,k = 1} when k ̸= j and by π when k = j. To recover
                                                                                            ′
the independence needed for concentration, we replace (ζ      b0,(−j) , Y              b0,(i ) , Y

                i ,·    i ,·
comparison Hessian is independent of Ωi′ ,· , and the expected weight of each summand remains
π, including that corresponding to k = j. Hence, the same matrix Bernstein argument
and population curvature bounds used above imply that its eigenvalues lie between constant
multiples of cψ πσr∗ and σ 2 πσ1∗ . Moreover, the leave-one-out error bounds and the same
perturbation decomposition as in (77) show that the operator-norm difference between the
original and comparison Hessians is o(cψ πσr∗ ). Invoking the fixed-pair concentration bound at
level 1 − O(d−c−2 ) and taking a union bound over (i′ , j) ∈ [n] × [p], Weyl’s inequality therefore
yields, uniformly over i′ ∈ [n] and j ∈ [p],

D     Gradient Descent from an ℓ2,∞ -Consistent Initialization
The final stage of the proposed pipeline applies Algorithm 1 to the one-step estimator. The pre-
ceding refinement provides a jointly aligned initialization that is accurate in both Frobenius and
rowwise norms. Our goal is to show that these guarantees persist along the gradient-descent tra-
jectory and, ultimately, yield the linear representations used for inference. The argument follows
an implicit-regularization strategy: rather than imposing explicit incoherence or balancing con-
straints, we prove that a suitably initialized trajectory remains in a region where the empirical loss
has stable local geometry.

D.1    Parameter-Explicit Pipeline Guarantees
We first state the parameter-explicit counterparts of Theorems 2.1 and 2.2. Unlike their main-text
specializations, the following results allow r, κ, and κψ to vary.

using the parameter-explicit tuning choices in Theorem B.1 and Lemma 5. For the theoretical

                    ∇X Ldiff = 4caug πXB(X, Y),               ∇Y Ldiff = −4caug πYB(X, Y).                (87)

                                                         73
The balancing term strengthens the curvature along the otherwise problematic scaling directions,
but it does not remove the rotational invariance of the factorization. Accordingly, the local cur-
vature result below is restricted to suitably aligned directions. We will show separately that the
original iterates remain approximately centered, incoherent, and balanced, so that the additional
gradient in (87) is negligible along the trajectory. This comparison transfers the contraction prop-
erties of the augmented dynamics to the actual iterates of Algorithm 1.

D.2     Auxiliary Results and Proofs of Theorems D.1 and D.2
We first collect the auxiliary results needed to prove Theorem D.1. The first result controls the local
geometry of the augmented loss. The second shows that the first two stages of the proposed pipeline
deliver the required initialization, and the third establishes the gradient-descent guarantees from an
arbitrary initialization satisfying these requirements. Their proofs are deferred to the subsequent
subsections. The general rowwise score-replacement lemmas used to deduce Theorem D.2 are
developed in Section D.7.

Remark 13. The Hessian is necessarily degenerate along rotational directions. Lemma 4 therefore

used in the contraction arguments below.

We fix an arbitrary measurable convention for the SVD; its orthogonal nonuniqueness is immaterial

   We now formalize the gradient-descent initialization requirements. In particular, we use the
radius ϵ = ϵ0 /κ2ψ from Lemma 4 to make the required RIC neighborhood explicit.

   Note that the actual input to Algorithm 1 may not fulfill (93) exactly, but the centering-and-
balancing map C in (92) ensures that the one after preprocessing in Algorithm 1 does. The following
lemma establishes the proximity between the input and the output of the centering-and-balancing
map, which is used to verify Assumption 9.

   The following theorem establishes the gradient-descent guarantees from a general warm start.
The proof is deferred to Section D.4.

The same conclusions hold with exceptional probability O(d−c−q ) for any fixed q ∈ {1, 2, 3}, after
increasing the fixed constants in the concentration bounds. Assumption 5, whose truncation event
has exceptional probability O(d−c−10 ), provides the required probability slack.
Proof of Theorem D.1. By Lemma 1, it suffices first to consider the balanced parametrization
ω = 1. The detailed spectral guarantee in Theorem B.1 verifies the global and leave-one-out con-
ditions required by Theorem C.1. Consequently, Lemma 5 shows that the centered-and-balanced
refactorization of the first two stages, together with its leave-one-out counterparts, satisfies Assump-
tion 9 and Condition 1 at t = 0. In particular, the initialization invoked in the gradient-descent
analysis is a consequence of the preceding pipeline rather than an additional assumption.
    We may therefore apply Theorem D.3. Equations (97), (98), and (99c) give the claimed bounds
when ω = 1. The weighted common-alignment criterion in Theorem D.1 is invariant under the
scaling equivalence. Thus the same common rotation applies for general ω, while Lemma 1 mul-
tiplies the X- and Y-errors by ω 1/4 and ω −1/4 , respectively, and leaves the intercept unchanged.
This proves the theorem.

    We use the following probability bookkeeping throughout this stage. Work first on the single
truncation event in Assumption 5, whose complement has probability O(d−c−10 ), and fix any q ∈
{0, 1, 2, 3}. Conditional on this event, the concentration bounds for a fixed iteration and a fixed
leave-one-out index are invoked with failure probability O(d−c−q−12 ). A union bound over l ∈
[n]∪[−p] therefore gives failure probability O(d−c−q−11 ) for each one-step result. The local-geometry
and sampling-operator events are uniform over the RIC and are selected at the same or a stronger
probability level, so they require no further union over the iterates. Since t0 ≤ d10 , a final union
bound over the gradient-descent steps retains failure probability O(d−c−q ). Adding the probability
of the shared truncation event does not change this order. Taking q = 0 yields the stated probability
in Theorem D.3; the stronger levels will be used below.
    Our analysis is based on an inductive argument. We assume that the bounds in Condition 1
hold at step t and show that they continue to hold at step t + 1 with high probability.

Remark 15. Several prior statistical analyses introduce explicit Frobenius-norm or balancing reg-
ularization into the actual or auxiliary nonconvex objective (Chen et al., 2019a; Fan et al., 2025a).
                                                      ⊤         ⊤
By contrast, we do not penalize the imbalance Xt Xt − Yt Yt , thereby avoiding the associated
regularization bias. Related optimization work shows that gradient methods can maintain or induce
approximate balancedness without such a penalty in asymmetric low-rank matrix factorization and
matrix sensing (Du et al., 2018; Ma et al., 2021; Ye and Du, 2021; Soltanolkotabi et al., 2023). Our
analysis requires a more stringent, inference-oriented form of this phenomenon: the Gram-matrix
discrepancy must remain sufficiently small throughout the trajectory that its contribution along the
scaling directions is negligible in the subsequent rowwise linear expansion and hence does not affect
the limiting distribution.

    We argue by induction over t. The initialization bounds (94), the explicit smallness conditions
(95), and Condition 1 at t = 0 establish the base case. Lemma 6 propagates the coupling be-
tween the actual and leave-one-out iterates, while Lemma 7 propagates the Frobenius and rowwise
estimation bounds. Lemma 8, in turn, keeps the iterates approximately balanced and centered.
    We make the RIC implication explicit. Since ν t ≤ 1, (95) makes the initialization-dependent
parts of the proximity bounds in Condition 1 smaller than half of the corresponding RIC radii with
ϵ = ϵ0 /κ2ψ . The signal-strength condition gives the same conclusion for the stochastic-error parts.
Consequently, uniformly over 0 ≤ t ≤ t0 ,
                                                      √ ∗
                     t ∗                   t t,(l)     ϵσr
             distF (θ , θ ) ∨ max distF (θ , θ ) ≤          ,
                             l∈[n]∪[−p]                2κ
                                                                                    r
                       t ∗                    t,(l) ∗                   t t,(l)    1 ϵσr∗
            dist2,∞ (θ , θ ) ∨ max dist2,∞ (θ , θ ) ∨ max distF (θ , θ ) ≤                 .
                               l∈[n]∪[−p]               l∈[n]∪[−p]                 2 κd

In the balanced parametrization, X∗ = Y∗ = σ1∗ and d = n ∨ p. Hence the second display
                                                      p

is stronger than each of the three rowwise inequalities in (91). The first display also verifies the
global proximity requirement in (90). The additional coupling bound and the triangle inequality
give the same conclusion when a leave-one-out iterate is aligned relative to the actual iterate. Thus
every aligned actual and leave-one-out iterate, as well as each line segment used in the one-step
argument, lies in the RIC. Lemma 4 therefore applies at the next iteration. Conditional on the
shared truncation event, the proofs of Lemmas 6, 7, and 8 may be run uniformly over all leave-
one-out indices with failure probability O(d−c−11 ) at each fixed iteration. Since t0 ≤ d10 , a union
bound over 0 ≤ t < t0 closes the induction with conditional failure probability O(d−c−1 ). Adding
the O(d−c−10 ) failure probability of the truncation event yields the stated probability 1 − O(d−c ).

                                                       88
    Recall that ν = 1−ηcHess πσr∗ /4. Since t0 ≥ ct log d/(ηcψ πσr∗ )−1, the inequality 1−x ≤ exp(−x)
gives ν t0 ≤ exp{ηcHess πσr∗ /4}d−ct cHess /(4cψ ) ≲ d−ct cHess /(4cψ ) . Together with σ ≥ d−c−10 from As-
sumption 5, this ensures, for a sufficiently large ct , that the initialization-dependent terms in the
contraction bounds are absorbed by their stochastic-error terms. Substituting t = t0 into Con-
dition 1 and using (111) therefore gives (99a)–(99c). The centering and balancedness conclusions
(99d)–(99e) follow by setting t = t0 in the last two inductive bounds.

    The same algebra applies to each leave-one-out objective L(l) . Replacing one row or column by
its population counterpart cannot increase the concentration bounds used above, and the initial-
ization is exactly centered and balanced for every l. For each fixed l, the conditional concentration
argument has failure probability O(d−c−2 ). Taking a union bound over l ∈ [n] ∪ [−p] proves the
two uniform leave-one-out bounds with failure probability O(d−c−1 ).

D.6     Analysis of the Auxiliary Sequence under the Augmented Objective
In addition to analyzing the actual GD iterates and their leave-one-out counterparts, we make a
detour to examine the vanilla gradient descent sequence under the augmented objective Laug , as
outlined in Section 2.4.2. Denote this sequence by θ aug,t := [ζ aug,t , Xaug,t , Yaug,t ]. It plays a crucial
role in establishing the approximate stationary condition in Section D.7. Formally, it is initialized
at θ aug,0 = θ 0 and updated according to θ aug,t = θ aug,t−1 − η∇θ Laug (θ aug,t−1 ). Correspondingly,
we introduce its leave-one-out variant {θ aug,t,(l) } with l ∈ [n] ∪ [−p] by θ aug,t,(l) = θ aug,t−1,(l) −


                                                         102
η∇θ L(l) (θ aug,t−1,(l) ) − η∇θ Ldiff (θ aug,t−1,(l) ) with L(l) defined in (109) and θ aug,0,(l) := θ 0,(l) . For its
comparison with the actual sequence, let
